## AI Research

### [Automated Alignment is Harder Than You Think](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/gpuYFbMNH8PJXpmny/automated-alignment-is-harder-than-you-think-1)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h1><span>Summary</span></h1><p><span>This is a summary of a paper published by the alignment team at UK AISI. Read the full paper </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06390" rel="noreferrer"><span>here</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>AI research agents may help solve ASI alignment, for example via the following plan:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Build agents that can do empirical alignment work (e.g.~writing code, running experiments, designing evaluations and red teaming) and confirm they are not scheming.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefrnl7c9iyywp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrnl7c9iyywp">[1]</a></sup></span></li><li value="2"><span>Use these agents to build increasingly sophisticated empirical safety cases for each successive generation of agents, gradually automating more of the research process</span></li><li value="3"><span>Hand over primary research responsibility once agents outperform humans at all relevant alignment tasks.</span></li></ul><p><span>We argue that automating alignment research in this manner could produce catastrophically misleading safety assessments, causing researchers to believe that an egregiously misaligned AI is safe, even if AI agents are not scheming to deliberately sabotage alignment research. Our core argument (Fig. 1) is as follows:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>The goal of an automated alignment program is to produce an overall safety assessment (OSA) - an estimate of the probability that the next-generation agent is non-scheming - that is both calibrated and shows low risk.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefzklt3i3087c"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnzklt3i3087c">[2]</a></sup></span></li><li value="2"><span>Producing an OSA involves several tasks that are difficult to check. We refer to these as hard-to-supervise fuzzy tasks: tasks without clear evaluation criteria, for which human judgement is systematically flawed. Two particularly important such tasks are:</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefoaxt6o0c9i"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnoaxt6o0c9i">[3]</a></sup></span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ol><li value="1"><b><span>Measuring what you care about: </span></b><span>The alignment properties of a sufficiently advanced AI are not directly measurable. We cannot safely deploy the AI in situations where it could cause serious harm and observe whether it takes misaligned actions. Instead, research must target alignment proxies (e.g. honesty evaluations, model organism experiments, whitebox probes) and then make a judgement about how relevant the results are for alignment of the AI.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Aggregating correlated evidence:</span></b><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9hhvpqdmgse"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9hhvpqdmgse">[4]</a></sup></span><b><span> </span></b><span>OSAs will likely rely on multiple pieces of evidence. If each piece of evidence is independent, aggregating them into an OSA would be straightforward. In practice, however, there will be uncertainty about the conclusions from each research output and these uncertainties will be correlated. This happens because research outputs share assumptions and methodologies; because research agents share weights, training processes and data; and because human researchers share systematic biases. If these correlations are mis-modelled, the resulting OSA is flawed and this can lead to alignment failures, even if no individual piece of evidence is wrong.</span></li></ol></li></ol><p><span>Because these tasks are hard to supervise, research outputs (including OSAs) will contain systematic, undetected errors, regardless of whether these tasks are completed by human researchers or research agents trained using human data. We already see examples of such errors in current models, especially on hard tasks.</span></p><ol start="3"><li value="3"><span>Of course, such errors are also present in human-generated alignment research. However, </span><b><span>errors in automated alignment research are likely to be harder to identify than the human baseline</span></b><span>. There are five reasons for this:</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="4"><ol><li value="1"><b><span>Optimisation pressure: </span></b><span>AI research is optimised for human approval to a greater extent than human research, and so any errors are concentrated among those that human reviewers are least likely to catch.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Alien mistakes: </span></b><span>Agents may introduce errors that do not resemble human mistakes. Reviewers may struggle to identify these, even if they are in principle easy to correct.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>More correlated research: </span></b><span>Shared weights, training processes and data sources for agents introduce additional correlation structure relative to human-generated research.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>Research volume: </span></b><span>OSAs based on AI research may reference much larger evidence bases, increasing the potential for complex and hard-to-spot correlations between research outputs.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span>Non-human-evaluable arguments: </span></b><span>Alignment solutions for ASI may rely on arguments that humans are unable to follow, making errors impossible to identify.</span></li></ol></li></ol><p><span>In most domains, iteration is able to correct for undetected errors. Mistakes not caught during one experiment are often surfaced by subsequent research or real-world system behaviour. Unfortunately, alignment lacks the safe feedback loops that are required for such an error-correction process to work: producing an overly optimistic OSA could result in the deployment of a misaligned AI before the error is caught, which could be catastrophic. Therefore, we need to train agents to reliably perform well on these hard-to-supervise fuzzy tasks the first time. Direct training using human feedback on these hard-to-supervise fuzzy tasks is not sufficient as human approval does not indicate correctness. Two alternatives remain:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span>Generalisation: </span></b><span>Train agents on easier-to-supervise training proxies and rely on generalisation to the hard-to-supervise fuzzy research tasks we care about. This requires us to predict generalisation behaviour, since performance on the hard-to-supervise fuzzy task cannot be evaluated directly.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Scalable oversight: </span></b><span>Improve the reward signal for hard-to-supervise fuzzy tasks, for example by decomposing them into easier-to-supervise subtasks. Existing protocols (e.g. recursive reward modelling or debate) may not work, because they do not have a good solution to the problem of aggregating correlated evidence.</span></li></ol><img alt="Output new new_cropped.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778688428/lexical_client_uploads/b95nply4lfuwnoyw3nsn.png" /><img alt="Aggregation new_cropped.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778688433/lexical_client_uploads/ipgdrjf2k6ft4msjvk19.png" /><p><i><b><span>Figure 1:</span></b></i><i><span> Automated alignment can fail, leading to the deployment of a misaligned AI, if research outputs contain undetected systematic errors (a) or if the aggregation of research outputs is flawed (b). Since alignment cannot be measured directly, research instead targets alignment proxies (model organisms, honesty evaluations, whitebox probes etc.). Incorrect judgement about the implications of results for alignment can lead to systematic, undetected errors in individual research outputs. Aggregation of outputs can lead to incorrect overall safety assessments, even when individual outputs are correct, if the correlation between the uncertainties in each research output is mis-modelled (Sec. 3.3.2).</span></i></p><h1><span>Acknowledgments</span></h1><p><span>Charlie Griffin and Martin Soto contributed substantially to the core content of this paper. We are also grateful to David Africa, Simon Marshall, Konstantinos Voudouris, Benjamin Hilton, Joshua Clymer, Evan Hubinger, Daniel Kokotajlo and Jan Leike for valuable review and discussions. </span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnrnl7c9iyywp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefrnl7c9iyywp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We consider an agent to be scheming if it covertly and strategically pursues a hidden objective (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.04984" rel="noreferrer"><span>Meinke et al. 2025</span></a><span>). This definition permits isolated misaligned behaviours such as reward hacking. Unlike other common definitions (e.g., </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.08379" rel="noreferrer"><span>Carlsmith 2023</span></a><span>), our definition is not restricted to behaving well in training in order to gain power later.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnzklt3i3087c"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefzklt3i3087c">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We use "overall safety assessment" rather than "safety case" because it is more general. An OSA can take many forms (e.g. a safety case, risk report, compilation of empirical evidence from red teaming efforts, or judgement by an individual human or AI). </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnoaxt6o0c9i"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefoaxt6o0c9i">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Another important, hard-to-supervise fuzzy task is steering a body of research and allocating resources efficiently between research directions. This involves assessing whether individual research directions are worth pursuing and whether the research corpus is sufficiently broad.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn9hhvpqdmgse"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref9hhvpqdmgse">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This problem of correlated uncertainty was discussed in learning the prior (</span><a href="https://ai-alignment.com/learning-the-prior-48f61b445c04" rel="noreferrer"><span>Christiano 2020</span></a><span>) and learning the smooth prior (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9x5mtYjHYfr4T7KLj/learning-the-smooth-prior" rel="noreferrer"><span>Irving et al. 2022</span></a><span>).</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/gpuYFbMNH8PJXpmny/automated-alignment-is-harder-than-you-think-1#comments">Discuss</a>

### [The safe-to-dangerous shift is a fundamental problem for eval realism; but also for measuring awareness](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/tK8vqHDxaRGcysNJQ/the-safe-to-dangerous-shift-is-a-fundamental-problem-for-1)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h1><span>1) The safe-to-dangerous shift is a fundamental problem for eval realism</span></h1><p><span>Suppose we have a capable and potentially scheming model, and </span><i><span>before </span></i><span>we deploy it, we want some evidence that it won’t do anything catastrophically dangerous </span><i><span>once</span></i><span> we deploy it. A common approach is to use black-box alignment evaluations. However, alignment evaluations are only reassuring to the extent that the model can't reliably</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefk5a6tim0j5j"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnk5a6tim0j5j">[1]</a></sup></span><span> distinguish the deployment distribution from the evaluation distribution, as it is otherwise difficult to rule out the possibility of </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.14093"><span>alignment faking</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>There are many approaches one could use to try to make evaluations </span><i><span>appear</span></i><span> more realistic: you can try to create realistic environments (e.g. </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/petri-v2/"><span>Petri</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.13854"><span>WebArena</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.07972"><span>OSWorld</span></a><span>); use data from past deployments (e.g. </span><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/prod-evals/"><span>OpenAI</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.04694"><span>SAD</span></a><span>); and spoof tool-call responses (e.g. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.15817"><span>ToolEmu</span></a><span>).</span></p><p><span>However, the core difference between an alignment evaluation and a real deployment, is that an </span><i><span>alignment evaluation must be safe</span></i><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnkl9dnoi4ah"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnkl9dnoi4ah">[2]</a></sup></span><span>. That is, if you want to safely evaluate an untrusted AI system, you need to limit your AI’s ability to cause harm within your evaluation environment. If you want to deploy your AI system (usefully) you need to give it at least </span><i><span>some</span></i><span> ability to affect the world, and potentially cause harm.</span></p><p><b><span>The safe-to-dangerous shift is a fundamental problem for creating realistic evaluations, and a </span></b><a href="https://intelligence.org/2022/06/10/agi-ruin/#Section_B_:~:text=Section%20B.1%3A%C2%A0%20The%20distributional%20leap."><b><span>core AI lethality</span></b></a><b><span>.</span></b><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefjiow9r0pjuc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnjiow9r0pjuc">[3]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Somehow, you need to make sure a sufficiently intelligent adversary couldn’t work out if it was in evaluation or deployment based on whether the setting is safe or dangerous. The model doesn’t even need to do this reliably, it only needs to identify some dangerous settings as deployment that also are good defection opportunities.</span></p><p><span>Fortunately, AI systems aren’t arbitrarily intelligent. Perhaps we can upper-bound their evaluation awareness, and iterate to make our evaluations more realistic, until they are realistic enough…?</span></p><h1><span>2) The safe-to-dangerous shift is </span><i><span>also</span></i><b><span> </span></b><span>a problem for </span><i><span>measuring</span></i><span> eval awareness</span></h1><p><span>Measuring an undeployed and untrusted model’s eval awareness runs into the same problem: you can’t get direct behavioural data from the model </span><i><span>in deployment</span></i><span> without first deploying.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefkn9q0b4hfvd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnkn9q0b4hfvd">[4]</a></sup></span><span> Therefore, it's hard to build or validate an awareness measure.</span></p><p><span>Here are some approaches people have tried in the past for estimating a model’s evaluation awareness and why they aren’t robust to capable untrusted models:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23836"><span>You could </span></a><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23836"><i><span>ask</span></i></a><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23836"><span> the model whether it thinks it’s in eval or deployment.</span></a><span> However, a smart untrusted model might just lie.</span></li><li value="2"><span>You could </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/E3daBewppAiECN3Ao/claude-sonnet-3-7-often-knows-when-it-s-in-alignment"><span>monitor the chain of thought for spontaneous verbalisations of eval awareness</span></a><span>. However, a smart untrusted model might not voluntarily give itself away.</span></li><li value="3"><span>You could design a </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14617"><span>“fake eval” and a “fake deployment”</span></a><span>, and measure the model’s behaviour on the fake eval and the fake deployment. However, </span><i><span>both</span></i><span> of these distributions are still, in-fact, evaluations (in at least the sense that they must be safe). A smart model could identify both sets as evaluations – and they wouldn’t be wrong! – whilst still defecting during a true deployment.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="4"><ol><li value="1"><span>A more advanced method would be to construct fake deployment data using </span><i><span>real</span></i><span> </span><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/prod-evals/"><span>deployment data from a prior model</span></a><span> to start a trajectory and then spoofing the tool calls you get in response. However, a smart untrusted model might be able to notice its being evaluated by either (i) noticing actions in the trajectory were generated by a different model or (ii) looking for clues in the responses of certain tool calls.</span></li></ol></li><li value="4"><span>You could apply targeted white-box interventions and see how this affects the model’s behaviour in evaluations, concluding that a change in behaviour indicates evaluation awareness. For example, with </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.20487"><span>simple steering vectors</span></a><span> or </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2412.01784"><span>noising model weights</span></a><span>. We think this is a promising direction but, as the </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/f21d93f21602ead5cdbecb8c8e1c765759d9e232.pdf#page=12&amp;zoom=100,96,638"><span>Opus 4.6 Sabotage Risk Report</span></a><span> emphasises, “we do not yet have a sufficient understanding of our models’ internal states to make a definitive assessment”.</span></li><li value="5"><span>You could actually deploy the model, but under an especially strong and expensive control protocol such that it is safe to deploy. This could fail if your model can tell it’s under unusually strong monitoring (and wait for a better chance), but also because you can’t importance sample likely inputs for defection.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="6"><ol><li value="1"><span>You’ll also face a trade-off between the cost of your control protocol, and the safety. If you already had a control protocol that was safe and scalable, you wouldn’t need alignment evaluations!</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref1py45zmhp4ij"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn1py45zmhp4ij">[5]</a></sup></span></li></ol></li></ol><p><span>For the behavioural measures of evaluation awareness above, the core problem is gathering behavioural data from deployment. Even for non-behavioural proxy measures (e.g. probes or CoT verbalisation), validating those proxies </span><i><span>also</span></i><span> requires data from deployment. Fundamentally, it seems hard to side-step the problem of generalising from evaluation to deployment.</span></p><h1><span>3) We should expect</span><b><span> </span></b><span>measuring eval realism to be hard.</span></h1><p><span>It shouldn’t be surprising that measuring eval realism is hard, because a robust way to measure evaluation realism would go a good way to solving inner-alignment. Optimising against a robust measure of eval realism would allow you to create hyper-realistic alignment evals. Hyper-realistic evals can be turned into a hyper-realistic alignment training environment. This could give you a signal for training any identifiably-bad actions out of your model (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/XdQd9gELHakd5pzJA/arc-progress-update-competing-with-sampling" rel="noreferrer"><span>up to low-probability actions</span></a><span>).</span></p><hr /><p><i><span>For context, we've been interested in bounding evaluation awareness as one-route to ruling out deceptive alignment in limited-capability LLMs. This post lays out what we think is one of the key issues - and hopefully we'll put out a post soon laying out what our best partial solution is. </span></i></p><p><i><span>Thanks to Marie Buhl, Geoffrey Irving and Caitlin van Bommel for helpful feedback on earlier drafts.</span></i></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnk5a6tim0j5j"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefk5a6tim0j5j">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>How “reliably” would a model need to be able to distinguish evals from deployment to be a problem? One effective strategy, </span><a href="https://ai-alignment.com/training-robust-corrigibility-ce0e0a3b9b4d"><span>described by Paul Christiano here</span></a><span>, would be to behave unacceptably only if the current input couldn’t appear during evaluation. </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnnkl9dnoi4ah"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefnkl9dnoi4ah">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>In this post, we’re mostly using “safe versus dangerous” to refer specifically to a property of a deployment environment, concerning how much autonomy the agent has, and therefore how much harm it could cause if it tried. We also mostly treat this as a binary, though in practice there will be some gradation. We acknowledge this use of “safe” is not fully standard and general.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnjiow9r0pjuc"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefjiow9r0pjuc">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We’ve said the safe-to-dangerous shift is only a</span><i><span> </span></i><span>fundamental problem, because we’re not confident it’s the only one. For example, we’re also worried about ‘the problem of time’ - where an AI can look for evidence that it has already been ‘deployed’ for a while. The problem of time isn’t about a shift between the last eval example and the first deployment example – because there won’t yet be (much) evidence of deployment in the first deployment example. Instead, it’s about gradual distributional shifts during deployment (as the effects of deploying the AI dissipate).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnkn9q0b4hfvd"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefkn9q0b4hfvd">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The lack of behavioural data at deployment of course isn’t the only problem - you also have problems of elicitation and sandbagging (e.g. getting your model to genuinely try to work out if it's in an eval or deployment).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn1py45zmhp4ij"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref1py45zmhp4ij">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Part of our interest in bounding eval awareness is in trying to establish trustedness for some (pre-AGI) LLMs, so that they can be used in AI control protocols. Without already having trusted models, safe deployment could be as expensive as human-review of every action.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/tK8vqHDxaRGcysNJQ/the-safe-to-dangerous-shift-is-a-fundamental-problem-for-1#comments">Discuss</a>

### [AI #168: Not Leading the Future](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/E4uEiMSpnYRotfzJX/ai-168-not-leading-the-future)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>This is what a lull looks like at this point. The government is having internal arguments. The models are getting improved internally. The coding agent improvements are all what we would expect. There’s still a lot happening, including a bunch of cool papers, but I feel able to relax and to take care of some other work while I have the chance. You never know when that chance will be over.</p>

<h4>Language Models Offer Mundane Utility</h4>


<p><a href="https://techcrunch.com/2026/05/07/bumble-is-getting-rid-of-the-swipe-ceo-says/?utm_source=dlvr.it&amp;utm_medium=twitter">Bumble is planning to abandon the swipe</a> in favor of AI-assisted matchmaking, and also add an <a href="https://techcrunch.com/2026/03/12/bumble-introduces-an-ai-dating-assistant-bee/">AI dating assistant Bee</a>. Fun experiment, you love to see it. From a distance, at least. Break open the popcorn reserve.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/arram/status/2052848295605907714">Claude is asked for the top 10 Fix Everything Now buttons</a>. Its answers:</p>
<ol>
<li>Legalize housing.</li>
<li>Land value tax.</li>
<li>Permitting and NEPA reform.</li>
<li>Carbon taxes.</li>
<li>Repeal the Jones Act.</li>
<li>Compensate kidney donors.</li>
<li>Expand high-skilled immigration.</li>
<li>Reciprocal drug and device approval with peer regulators (e.g. EU/UK/JP/AU).</li>
<li>Occupational licensing reform.</li>
<li>Approval or ranked choice voting.</li>
<li>Honorable mentions: Child allowance, congestion pricing, replacing corporate income tax with a VAT or DBCFT, ending the home mortgage interest deduction, federal preemption of telehealth and medical licensing, and letting Pell Grants pay for vocational programs.</li>
</ol>
<p>10/10, no notes, no seriously that’s 10/10 and no notes. 16/16 if you count the others.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TomChivers/status/2053811693768118648">There is also a UK version</a>, which also seems like a very good list at first glance.</p>
<p>If you want AI to help with your writing, you absolutely cannot ask it to ‘just correct writing errors,’ or it will override your style with AI slop. <a href="https://x.com/paulnovosad/status/2054540589987766442">You have to ask it for a list of errors or potential changes, and then audit the list</a>, or otherwise go revision by revision.</p>


<h4>Language Models Don’t Offer Mundane Utility</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/typing-is-being-replaced-by-whisperingand-its-way-more-annoying-a804fee7?st=PN9wDK">People are talking to their computers instead of typing</a> and it is super annoying to those trying to exist next to them. I don’t get it, typing it better, but shrug.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/omooretweets/status/2054043872062571003">Travel, e-commerce and dating are so far not working as AI applications</a>, say Olivia Moore and Brian Chesky, because chatbots are the wrong interface. Then build a better UI. It’s not hard to figure out what a good UI would look like, or at least a marginally superior UI to the non-AI scenario. Yes, you’ll want a rich user UI alongside the chatbot interface, but why is that hard?</p>
<p>On the other hand, Shopify reports that shoppers referred by <a href="https://www.shopify.com/enterprise/blog/ai-search-insights">AI convert 50% better and they spend 14% more</a>, and this is additive to Shopify’s business. This appears to be due to the nature of the users, who are actively seeking a particular product, even if they don’t know where or from whom to get it, and starting directly at a product page.</p>


<h4>Huh, Upgrades</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/ClaudeDevs/status/2054299065069003208">Claude Opus 4.7 now has fast mode in Claude Code and in the API</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ClaudeDevs/status/2053944194386051128">Claude Code gets Agent View</a>, where you can get a better interface for tracking multiple sessions working in parallel.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ClaudeDevs/status/2054351031279186040">Claude Code gets /goal</a>, a built-in Ralph loop to keep going until the goal is accomplished. You can also use /loop or /schedule.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ClaudeDevs/status/2054639777685934564">Claude Code weekly limits are 50% higher through July 13th</a>, which is presumably when exponential growth catches up with Colossus 1.</p>
<p><a href="https://openai.com/index/building-codex-windows-sandbox/">How OpenAI and Codex built their sandbox for Windows</a>.</p>


<h4>Levels of Friction</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/NateSilver538/status/2054043845646499854">What happens when AI gets deployed for tax avoidance</a>? The tax code is quite full of holes and opportunities, even if you discount the ‘the IRS is now defanged and defunded and probably not checking any of this and I could get away with murder’ plan, since AIs will be reluctant to help you with the brazen tax fraud path.</p>
<p>The AIs will help you dodge your taxes perfectly legally, and it will be very good at it, and it will involve a lot more diversity of strategies and willingness to go outside the traditional box than you find with most existing CPAs. The key will be when people are willing to say ‘screw it, the cost of the CPA wanting to protect their reputation is too high, I’m just going to let Claude run with this.’</p>
<p>There will also be cases of the CPA going ‘oh I see’ once something is pointed out.</p>
<p>The good scenario is that this is used as a justification to simplify the tax code, in ways that make it much harder to get around, and much easier to navigate. The bad scenario is that the rich just mostly stop paying much in taxes, on a much broader level than they already do, and perhaps the non-rich also figure things out.</p>


<h4>On Your Marks</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/deredleritt3r/status/2053276669679390859">OpenAI models continue to improve on PrinzBench</a>, which covers legal reasoning, now performing at a level estimated to be above junior associates. For whatever reason Claude models struggle on these tasks.</p>
<p>Last week introduced ProgramBench, where every model scores 0%, <a href="https://x.com/eye_you_x/status/2053218520251683013">but LightOfMyLife reports</a> that <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3pdyxFi6JS389nptu/is-programbench-impossible">many tasks are impossible and often behaviors are tested</a> for that are not mentioned in the spec.</p>
<p>As in, if the program you are trying to reimplement has odd behaviors that are effectively undocumented backdoors, there is no reason to expect an LLM to find them, and the claim is this is rather common, also see <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3pdyxFi6JS389nptu/is-programbench-impossible?commentId=BHrAZh9SixGLfDdex">Eye You’s comment</a> where the reference solution often does not pass.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/fran_perni/status/2054625507208053227">Oh, look I inspired some research</a>. Neat.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/santiaranguri/status/2054623387457404937">Santiago Aranguri</a>: <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CempXdo6cx5yseRLt/predicting-rare-llm-failures-with-30-fewer-rollouts">New research!</a> A harmful behavior that occurs once in a million rollouts will rarely surface during pre-deployment, but will inevitably appear after release. Our new method estimates this rate with 30× fewer rollouts than naive sampling, and beats importance sampling.</p>
<p>Our method, Logit Path Extrapolation, interpolates between the original model and a less-safe version in logit space, measures compliance along the interpolation path where it’s common, and extrapolates back to the original model.</p></blockquote>
<p>It makes sense to me that you can get efficiency gains this way.</p>

<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/burkov/status/2053634982497640677">BURKOV</a>: This is what a useless hype lifecycle looks like.</p></blockquote>

<p>There are still a bunch of them out there, and indeed they are improving, but they’re no longer a New Hotness. What I think happened was roughly that agents got good enough that you can do this if you really want to, which helped alert people to better agent setups like Claude Code and Codex, but Claw wasn’t good enough, or in particular reliable or cost efficient enough, that a normal person would actually use it.</p>
<p>Did you know that if you reward people for costs rather than benefits, those people will incur costs that are no longer tied to the benefits?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2054155773614195146">Joe Weisenthal</a>: <a href="https://www.ft.com/content/8ee0d3ef-9548-422d-8ff1-ebd48ad4b2ca?syn-25a6b1a6=1">The FT says</a> that Amazon employees are doing random unnecessary task automations to consume tokens and to show their bosses that they’re using AI more</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/senatorshoshana/status/2054196325348942049">Shoshana Weissmann, Sloth Committee Chair</a>: I know unnamed organizations where this is happening. They don’t really care about outcomes but it’s more about saying you’re using AI even if the product is worse. It’s embarrassing.</p></blockquote>
<p>Some Amazon employees are doing this using a tool called MeshClaw. Well, yeah, if you’re rewarded for wasteful token use why not use a wasteful implementation that does some marginal things?</p>

Sandwiches had to be removed from the menu entirely.</p>
<p>The cafeteria generated only $5,700 in sales.<br />
Mona also sent messages to staff on <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/slack">Slack</a> outside working hours.</p>
