### [Models finding software vulnerabilities is not the primary source of cybersecurity risk](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/gutiw8MBrYDiD2u5z/models-finding-software-vulnerabilities-is-not-the-primary)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>I have tried and failed to write a longer post since 2024, so here goes a short one with less detail.</span></p><p><span>Discourse has primarily focused on models' ability to develop new exploits against important software from scratch. That capability is impressive, but the tech industry has been dealing with people regularly finding 0-day exploits for important pieces of software for more than </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pwn2Own" rel="noreferrer"><span>twenty years</span></a><span>.  Having to patch these vulnerabilities at a 10xed or even 100xed cadence for a fixed period of time is well within the resources of Mozilla, the Linux Foundation, and Microsoft. The lag time between "patch shipped" and "patch reverse engineered and weaponized by a criminal organization" was already so long that most people didn't notice new bugs when they came out. And such capabilities are dual use; defenders already have access to them and will be using the models to prevent their engineers from releasing new bugs. </span></p><p><span>There are lots of capabilities that are not like this, however:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Weaponizing recently patched exploits for common software.</span></b><span> Right now, for widely used C projects, we get enough publicly disclosed vulnerabilities to develop exploits with. Every amateur computer hacker has had the experience of seeing a CVE for a version number currently in use by a service, getting excited, and being surprised when it's totally useless. </span><br /><br /><span>Part of that is because lots of CVEs are inflated, but part of it is just that modern memory protections mean that it's months of hard work to actually exploit these "high" severity CVEs for your favorite product. But AI reduces that down to hours, and </span><a href="https://web.archive.org/web/20260512174712/https://github.com/g00dfe11ow/Shai-Hulud-Open-Source" rel="noreferrer"><span>will even help you with worm development if you want</span></a><span> . If the output of "stop-the-presses" vulnerabilities for SaaS vendors slows down to its 2025 cadence, but each such vulnerability is now exploited in the wild as soon as open source projects ship a patch, that seems like a lasting loss for defense.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>AI-enabled social engineering.</span></b><span> Right now black hat groups can only run really sophisticated, long term compromise attempts on Guillermo Rauch. But when the bottleneck for conducting such attacks is no longer labor, and the price of intelligence gets low enough, they can and will begin elaborate campaigns to scam and hack more ordinary people. The ease with which attackers will soon be able to generate high fidelity internet legacies, and the amount of apparent effort they will be able to put in creating relationships with marks, will break a lot of peoples' assumptions about trust over the internet at the same time - especially normies who do not read or care much about AI, and might be behind the times on what people can do. </span></li><li value="3"><b><span>"Post-exploitation", or "the stuff attackers do after they already have access to a given target and can begin exploiting their resources, public projects, etc."</span></b><span>, is IMO the most important risk. There is a fundamental logistical problem that every botnet operator throughout history has run into called "command and control", or roughly, "how to maintain control over and reap rewards from all of the stuff you've pwned." It used to be that the worst thing these people could do was launch DDoS attacks against a particular target, or send spam email, or scrape for crypto and credit cards, because even if you manage to create a worm that spreads over the internet, you can't personally review every individually owned laptop for goodies. </span><br /><br /><span>But if you put your sociopath goggles on, the average software engineer sure has access to a lot of software repositories that </span><i><span>could in principle</span></i><span> be leveraged for further attacks, along with many online accounts &amp; emails with which to abuse trust relationships. With sufficiently intelligent post exploitation, an AI could probably hop from that software engineer to &gt;2 close friends with similar access and keep going, actively hacking each target until a large percentage of the wider graph is compromised. And once it is, AIs can be a lot more strategic &amp; effective about making money from each target in the most profitable way. </span></li></ul><p><span>These vectors, which have already gotten worse over the last six months, will become a more pressing issue over the next 24 months, and are more important causes for concern than vulnerability research, in part because they have no obvious solutions like vulnerability research does.</span></p><p><span>[Thanks to Chris Hacking (real name) for talking through some of these ideas in conversation with me]</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/gutiw8MBrYDiD2u5z/models-finding-software-vulnerabilities-is-not-the-primary#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Claude is Now Alignment-Pretrained](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Xqh9bDw7Ei5bExC6h/claude-is-now-alignment-pretrained-1)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Anthropic are now actively using the approach to alignment often called “</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/alignment-pretraining" rel="noreferrer"><span>Alignment Pretraining</span></a><span>” or “Safety Pretraining” — using Stochastic Gradient Descent on a large body of natural or synthetic documents showing the AI assistant doing the right thing in morally challenging situations. They tried this out, found it works well and generalizes well, and they’re now using it.</span></p><p><span>I’m absolutely delighted. I’ve been repeatedly advocating this approach on LessWrong and the Alignment Forum for a couple of years now:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/JviYwAk5AfBR7HhEn/how-to-control-an-llm-s-behavior-why-my-p-doom-went-down-1"><span>How to Control an LLM's Behavior (why my P(DOOM) went down)</span></a></li><li value="2"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RCoBrvWfBMzGWNZ4t/motivating-alignment-of-llm-powered-agents-easy-for-agi-hard"><span>Motivating Alignment of LLM-Powered Agents: Easy for AGI, Hard for ASI?</span></a></li><li value="3"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/oRQMonLfdLfoGcDEh/a-bitter-lesson-approach-to-aligning-agi-and-asi-1"><span>A "Bitter Lesson" Approach to Aligning AGI and ASI</span></a></li><li value="4"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/XdpJsY6QGdCbvo2dS/why-aligning-an-llm-is-hard-and-how-to-make-it-easier" rel="noreferrer"><span>Why Aligning an LLM is Hard, and How to Make it Easier</span></a></li><li value="5"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xAsviBJGSBBtgBiCw/the-best-way-to-align-an-llm-is-inner-alignment-now-a-solved" rel="noreferrer"><span>The Best Way to Align an LLM: Is Inner Alignment Now a Solved Problem?</span></a></li><li value="6"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RCoBrvWfBMzGWNZ4t/motivating-alignment-of-llm-powered-agents-easy-for-agi-hard"><span>Motivating Alignment of LLM-Powered Agents: Easy for AGI, Hard for ASI?</span></a></li><li value="7"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ZeWewFEefCtx4Rj3G/pretraining-on-aligned-ai-data-dramatically-reduces" rel="noreferrer"><span>Pretraining on Aligned AI Data Dramatically Reduces Misalignment—Even After Post-Training</span></a></li></ul><p><span>I’ve been very excited about this alignment technique ever since I read the seminal paper demonstrating that it was extremely effective: </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2302.08582.pdf"><i><span>Pretraining Language Models with Human Preferences</span></i></a><span> (Korbak et al., ’23). This was later followed up by </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2504.16980"><i><span>Safety Pretraining: Toward the Next Generation of Safe AI</span></i></a><span> (Maini, Goyal, Sam et al., ’25), </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.05475"><i><span>You Are What You Eat - AI Alignment Requires Understanding How Data Shapes Structure and Generalisation</span></i></a><span> (Lehalleur, Hoogland, Farrugia-Roberts et al., ’25), and most recently </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2601.10160v1" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>Alignment Pretraining: AI Discourse Causes Self-Fulfilling (Mis)alignment</span></i></a><span> (Tice, Radmard, et al., ’26).</span></p><p><span>Others have also posted on LessWrong on this subject, such as  </span><a href="https://turntrout.com/about"><span>TurnTrout</span></a><span>’s </span><a href="https://turntrout.com/self-fulfilling-misalignment"><span>Self-Fulfilling Misalignment Data Might Be Poisoning Our AI Models</span></a><span>,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefj8xps125o3c"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnj8xps125o3c">[1]</a></sup></span><span> and </span><a href="https://www.beren.io/aboutme/"><span>Beren Millidge</span></a><span>’s </span><a href="https://www.beren.io/2024-05-11-Alignment-in-the-Age-of-Synthetic-Data/"><span>Alignment In The Age Of Synthetic Data</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/XnnMYMjDGuYhkhQPB/the-case-for-removing-alignment-and-ml-research-from-the"><span>The case for removing alignment and ML research from the training data</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://www.beren.io/2023-07-05-My-path-to-prosaic-alignment-and-open-questions/"><span>My path to prosaic alignment and open questions</span></a><span>. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/nostalgebraist?from=post_header" rel="noreferrer"><span>Nostalgebraist</span></a><span> discussed something closely related in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3EzbtNLdcnZe8og8b/the-void-1"><span>the void</span></a><span>, as did </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/seth-herd?from=search_autocomplete"><span>Seth Herd</span></a><span> in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/oveSZmWHjFQbgR4Nn/broadening-the-training-set-for-alignment"><span>Broadening the training set for alignment</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>Anthropic are also specifically using fiction in which Claude does the right thing in difficult moral situations as training material, so they have even implemented my suggestion of </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/aligned-ai-role-model-fiction"><span>Aligned AI Role-Model Fiction</span></a><span>, which was also taken up by </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/jayterwahl?from=post_header"><span>Aaron Silverbook</span></a><span>/</span><a href="https://www.hyperstitionai.com/"><span>Hyperstition AI</span></a><span>: see their posts </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9NntwpQj9onbEFM8L/silicon-morality-plays-the-hyperstition-progress-report-1"><span>Silicon Morality Plays: The Hyperstition Progress Report</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/hkzw97Y73yWMS7BFd/special-persona-training-hyperstition-progress-report-2"><span>Special Persona Training: Hyperstition Progress Report 2</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>So I’m happy this idea’s time has finally come.</span><br /><br /><br /></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnj8xps125o3c"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefj8xps125o3c">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This post, like some of the other early discussion on this concept on LessWrong, focused primarily on suggesting we should keep examples of AIs behaving badly out of the training set. Anthropic have instead concentrated on the opposite: putting examples of LLMs behaving well into the training set. This is fully consistent with the academic papers on the subject, three of which tied both of these approaches and uniformly found that increasing good examples is much more important than reducing bad examples.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Xqh9bDw7Ei5bExC6h/claude-is-now-alignment-pretrained-1#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Cyber Lack of Security and AI Governance](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pzjGXSKrkHGdGbtrW/cyber-lack-of-security-and-ai-governance)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>The real recent story of AI has been the background work being done on Cybersecurity, as we process the Mythos Moment along with GPT-5.5, and figure out both how to patch the internet and what our new regulatory regime is going to look like.</p>
<p>The Trump Administration is being dragged, kicking and screaming, into the era of at least some situational awareness, and acknowledgment that catastrophic risks are very much a real risk and they need to have a role in supervising frontier model releases. Now that they’re there, Commerce is deciding who gets access to the most powerful model in the world, and they are fighting Intelligence and the national security state over who should be in charge.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25283"></span>


</div>
<p>Another question is, exactly how strong is Mythos, both compared to past model and to GPT-5.5 and also in absolute terms? We got multiple new reports on that, as well as the METR graph results. There’s little question Mythos is a big deal, but there’s a wide range of big deals out there.</p>
<p>Part of the new report from UK AISI is learning that there is a substantial gap between the abilities of the early Mythos Preview (Mythos Preview Preview?) that UK AISI originally reviewed, versus the final version. One would expect more continuous improvement is going on, invisibly to us, in the background.</p>


<h4>Table of Contents</h4>


<ol>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/197563764/on-your-marks">On Your Marks.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/197563764/how-good-is-mythos">How Good Is Mythos?</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/197563764/cyber-lack-of-security">Cyber Lack of Security.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/197563764/greetings-from-the-department-of-war">Greetings From The Department of War.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/197563764/the-prior-restraint-era-begins">The Prior Restraint Era Begins.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/197563764/commerce-versus-intelligence">Commerce Versus Intelligence.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/197563764/the-quest-for-sane-regulations">The Quest for Sane Regulations.</a></li>
</ol>


<h4>On Your Marks</h4>


<p>It is difficult to fully fill the METR.</p>
<p>At 50% success rates, Mythos is above the threshold where METR’s methodology is reliable, which tells us very little since that result is on trend.</p>
<p>At 80% success rates, there are enough tasks where models remain unreliable that the result is still within measuring range. This shows Mythos is modestly above trend, in addition to likely having been somewhat more delayed than usual.</p>
<p>At 95% success rate, no model can get much of a score, because of a subset of tasks, even quick ones, where models struggle. Again, this is an artifact of the particular set of tasks selected.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/METR_Evals/status/2052896621760004602">METR</a>: We evaluated an early version of Claude Mythos Preview for risk assessment during a limited window in March 2026. We estimated a 50%-time-horizon of at least 16hrs (95% CI 8.5hrs to 55hrs) on our task suite, at the upper end of what we can measure without new tasks.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2053169880413999586">Peter Wildeford</a>: Deep learning is hitting a wall (the wall being our ability to measure AI capabilities)</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/pzjGXSKrkHGdGbtrW/cghndumwfriy2jbb0d9d" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/pzjGXSKrkHGdGbtrW/ua3dl7eiw6s6igo7ztbe" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p><a href="https://x.com/krishnakaasyap/status/2052905151086858523">Krishna Kaasyap</a>: I still don’t think this eval is saturated!</p>
<p>At an 80% success rate, Mythos is still under 4 hours. Well within task distribution.<br />
At a 99% success rate, Mythos is still under 5 fricking minutes! Long live the Task-Completion Time Horizons eval!</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/GaryMarcus/status/2053177641432404005">Gary Marcus</a>: Sorry, @peterwildeford , but this is wrong. Please don’t play along. The measurement “wall” you mention is hit ONLY if you don’t insist on reliability.</p>
<p>If you demanded 95% accuracy on the task, the systems wouldn’t be close to the measurement wall. The measurement problem you allude to is an artifact of artificially lowered expectations.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/GaryMarcus/status/2053177057660805132">Gary Marcus</a>: That wall would not apply at 95% reliability. Probably not even close. Accepting a fair amount of error lowers the bar.</p></blockquote>
<p>It is an important fact about current LLMs that there are some tasks, even short tasks, they are unable to do. It is another important fact that for a wide range of tasks, even some very long tasks, they can now do them, that group is expanding, and for each task in this group they are increasingly reliable.</p>
<p>If a model can do a task at all, you can probably scaffold it into doing it reliably, up to some very high probability of success, so long as you have a validator for the result.</p>
<p>On Palisade Research’s SelfReplicateBench (not that they call it that), we see <a href="https://x.com/PalisadeAI/status/2052587437315305830">models making huge jumps in being able to hack their way to chains of self-replication</a>, when <a href="https://palisaderesearch.org/blog/self-replication">given access to targets that lacked strong defenses</a>.</p>
<p>To be clear, yes they explicitly gave these models a system instruction to be fully autonomous, target intentionally exploitable systems in eval mode, and to aim for replication. This is not something that happened by accident. But do not doubt that people, given the opportunity, will explicitly instruct similar things in the real world, even if you don’t think the AIs will ever decide to do this on their own for other reasons such as instrumental convergence.</p>

<h4>How Good Is Mythos?</h4>


<p>Mythos is quite good, and even more importantly tomorrow’s Mythos (or GPT) will be better still. Capabilities will continue to advance, and indeed they already have substantially improved from the verison UK AISI initially tested.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2054635025791131819">Dean W. Ball</a>: In life, everything is a wager. Whether you realize it or not, you are constantly making implicit and explicit predictions about the future state of reality. To live is to predict.</p>
<p>So when you are faced with something like Mythos, and you say, “this is just ‘doomer hype’!,” what you are really doing is making a bet against model capabilities growth, and thus ultimately you are making a broad directional bet against deep learning, which has usually been a pretty bad bet to make.</p>
<p>I am surprised that so many people—people who are otherwise AI optimists!—continue to make these bets against deep learning. They keep being wrong, and the less humble among them have torched their credibility with anyone paying attention.</p>
<p>So ask yourself, when you make claims about AI and its future: “am I making an implicit bet against deep learning in a broad directional way?”</p></blockquote>
<p>The rest of this section is an update on the Mythos we have today. There are two new reports on the capabilities of Mythos, and they affirm as expected that compute has not been a limiting factor, so that excuse for the White House denying expanded access does not hold water, especially now that Anthropic has Colossus 1.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AISecurityInst/status/2054589763173126339">Note that the new UK AISI results are of the final Mythos preview</a>, which is a substantial step up from the preliminary version they previously tested.</p>

<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>Here’s XBOW’s results:</p>

<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/pzjGXSKrkHGdGbtrW/ceqvtlyu1reeqow07t7b" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/pzjGXSKrkHGdGbtrW/zqmzmcyonk63a0bxc03y" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>Our key takeaways after analyzing Mythos Preview include:</p>
<ul>
<li>It’s extremely powerful for source code audits.</li>
<li>It’s good, but less powerful, at validating exploits.</li>
<li>Its judgment is mixed. It can be too literal and conservative, and also tends to overstate the practical relevance of its findings.</li>
<li>It’s strong in native-code vulnerability discovery and reverse engineering.</li>
</ul>
<p>The overall picture is that GPT-5.5 is a big jump, Mythos is a very big jump, and there is a substantial gap from GPT-5.5 to Mythos but yes both are big deals. In addition to that, I believe Mythos has an ability to ‘put it all together’ at scale that GPT-5.5 does not fully share, making it a bigger practical advantage than the tests indicate. But yes, GPT-5.5 would be a really big deal on its own.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/logangraham/status/2054613618168082935">Logan Graham</a> (Head of Glasswing, Anthropic): A lot of people have been wondering about Mythos, Glasswing, and the vulns we / our partners are fixing. Today, I’m excited for us to start sharing more.</p>
<p>Two independent evaluations this week—<a href="https://t.co/Mumtbf3kE3">from XBOW</a> and <a href="https://t.co/vBgqz0AeKJ">the UK AISI</a>—confirm what we’ve been seeing internally: Claude Mythos Preview is a step change in autonomous cybersecurity capabilities. We need to start preparing fast for a world of models with this level of capabilities.</p>
<p>The UK AI Security Institute tested the model we shipped at the launch of Project Glasswing and found Mythos Preview is the first model to solve both of their end-to-end cyber ranges, including one (Cooling Tower) which no model had ever cleared. But attackers (and defenders) have sophistication &amp; cost constraints – Mythos is also the only model that clears every one of their tasks estimated over 8 hours under their deliberately low 2.5M-token cap.</p>
<p>XBOW tested it on their offensive security benchmarks, finding “token-for-token, unprecedented precision.” It’s the only model to succeed at subtle V8 sandbox work.</p>
<p>Other Glasswing partners shared similar stories. In a few weeks of testing, Mythos Preview has helped them find many thousands of (estimated) high + critical severity vulnerabilities, sometimes double what they’d normally find in a year.</p>
<p>… We started Project Glasswing because capabilities like Mythos Preview’s won’t stay rare, or stay in careful hands. We are bringing it to defenders as fast as we responsibly can, while working to figure out, for example, the right safeguards and patching &amp; disclosure processes.</p>
<p>Also, to be clear, compute has never been a limiter in our rollout. Expect a fuller update on our Glasswing work in the coming days.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AISecurityInst/status/2054589763173126339">AI Security Institute</a>: In AISI’s latest testing, the newer Mythos Preview checkpoint completed both our cyber ranges, solving the range “The Last Ones” in 6 of 10 attempts and the previously unsolved “Cooling Tower” in 3 of 10 attempts. This was the first time that a model completed the second of our two cyber ranges. GPT-5.5 solved “The Last Ones” on 3 of 10 attempts.</p>
<p>These results utilise a newer Mythos Preview checkpoint than that included in previous AISI reporting. Notable capability jumps do not always require new model releases: later iterations of the same model can also meaningfully change our estimates of frontier capabilities.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Cyber Lack of Security</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/WeinbaumJonah/status/2054603215820161475">Mythos took us by surprise in large part because CAISI is a $15 million pilot program</a>, which leaves it severely underfunded. It needs a lot more than that to do its job. This post say $84 million, which would be a big help. I say it should be a lot more.</p>
<p><a href="https://openai.com/daybreak/">OpenAI gives us Daybreak</a>, which is their version of Project Glasswing.</p>
<p><a href="https://hackerone.com/anthropic">Anthropic has a live bug bounty program on HackerOne</a>.</p>
<p>Germany moves to form an AISI <a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2053843171587891376">and demands access to Mythos</a>. There is some amount of ‘you use regulations against our technology firms and now here you are demanding access’ but also my (non-confident) understanding is that Anthropic wants to give Germany and others access and it is our government that is vetoing that, and doing so largely out of spite.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2052826481772855509">Palo Alto Security says Mythos found a year’s worth of penetration methods</a> <a href="https://www.paloaltonetworks.com/blog/2026/05/frontier-ai-defense/">in three weeks</a>, and says this next wave of models increases coding efficiency 50%.</p>
<p><a href="https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/ai-vulnerability-exploitation-initial-access?utm_source=tw&amp;utm_medium=social&amp;utm_campaign=nfg">Google reports they have found a threat actor</a> using a known-to-be AI-developed zero-day exploit in the wild.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/MTSlive/status/2052446734950035928">The IMF joins</a> those warning about AI-enabled cyberattacks in the wake of Mythos.</p>
<p><a href="https://hacks.mozilla.org/2026/05/behind-the-scenes-hardening-firefox/">Firefox explains how they went from</a> ‘AI bug discoveries are worthless slop’ to ‘AI finds tons of critical bugs and we fix them,’ including building their own harness.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/pzjGXSKrkHGdGbtrW/oymf7if3u2cbrbwiako4" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/DKThomp/status/2052484462739706115">Derek Thompson</a>: Skepticism of corporate marketing and AI boosterism is always warranted, but I think the folks who accused Anthropic of overrating Mythos should check out this post by Mozilla developers indicating that the Firefox team fixed more security bugs in April using Mythos than in the past 15 months combined.</p></blockquote>
<p>There should be zero skepticism that there has been an overall step change in cyber capabilities. One could still object that GPT-5.5 plus a similarly good harness and spending campaign could have done much of the same job. I think that would have fallen well short of what we got, but it would still have been an acceleration of past efforts, and probably a large one.</p>
<p>For now, the practical cyber threats are usually more pedestrian, and are <a href="https://x.com/vxunderground/status/2052558711240245445">things like ShinyHunters doing standard data exfiltration and ransom</a> techniques, which is a place where hardening could help. We are still in the calm before the storm.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/RyanPGreenblatt/status/2053304865540186153">Ryan Greenblatt moderates his estimates of how much damage</a> Mythos would have caused if released into the wild, due to defenders having to scramble into emergency mode. It is a reasonable position to suggest that because People Don’t Do Things, whereas defenders in crisis mode actually do things, it might not be that bad, and things might not break down so much in general. This is possible, but there is quite the long tail involved, and I am very glad we are never going to find out.</p>
<p>If any new code can be attacked by AI on the spot, <a href="https://x.com/jd_pressman/status/2053900617740075065">your subsequent patching will be slower than the attackers</a>. You’ll need to test every deployment and patch for vulnerabilities, at the same level as it will be probed afterwards, prior to deployment. Are we prepared to do that? We need to be for anything we care enough about not being compromised. 90 day disclosure windows will soon be at least 89 days too long.</p>


<h4>Greetings From The Department of War</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.axios.com/2026/05/07/pentagon-tech-chief-no-anthropic-resolution?utm_campaign=mrf-utm_campaign=editorial&amp;utm_source=x&amp;utm_medium=owned_social&amp;mrfcid=2026050769f26342d2e5ee7a5a3f4014">Emil Michael is resolute </a>that no, they will never use Anthropic again, oh no no, it must always be ‘all lawful use’ and any asking of questions or having opinions or morals will never be tolerated, and look no one else is going to dare ask questions or have opinions or morals.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/ashleyrgold/status/2052420562996150318">Ashley Gold</a>: . @USWREMichael , speaking at @scsp_ai conf, says Anthropic won’t be added to list of AI companies striking deals with Pentagon. “Never again” will Pentagon be “single threaded” on one vendor &amp; list of companies shows how many are willing to work with them in “all lawful purposes.”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Miles_Brundage/status/2052440142955303003">Miles Brundage</a>: Really don’t understand the situation/endgame here</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2052442141255938452">Dean W. Ball</a>: The lawyers told him that saying “we could always still make a deal” and “they are a threat to the military on the level of a firm controlled or linked to a foreign-adversary’s military” was a bad idea.</p></blockquote>
<p>And then he got back to using Claude Gov and also Mythos.</p>
<p>The situation and endgame is that at some point Pete Hegseth and Emil Michael will leave the Department of War. Until then, they’re going to keep up this line, probably, while in practice they probably use Anthropic products anyway.</p>
<p>Or if not, whatever, it’s not like Anthropic needs the business or frankly the trouble, and they’ll be here if you change your mind. If I was Anthropic I’d want the supply chain risk designation lifted but I wouldn’t want any part of the Department of War except to assist the transition out, at minimum until there was a change of leadership.</p>


<h4>The Prior Restraint Era Begins</h4>


<p>Doing anything ‘like the FDA’ is not the change we want to see in the world, but it is change, and that it is being talked about on CNBC by the admin is clear confirmation that given sufficient impetus policy stances can change rapidly. What would previously have been unthinkable and gotten you run out of town on a rail as a ‘degrowther’ or ‘doomer’ or what not suddenly is floated by the White House.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2052529057464525026">Nate Soares</a> (MIRI): I’ve been asked a few times for my take on how the White House is considering reviewing AI models “like an FDA drug” before release. My main take is: When people start to recognize the dangers, policy stances can turn on a dime. There’s hope.</p></blockquote>
<p>One problem with having spent the last three years treating all potential regulations as unthinkable is the failure to think about them, which is why the proposals being floated were such terrible implementations.</p>
<p>Thus, safety advocates can be happy that we may finally be getting some meaningful oversight, but most of us are rather apprehensive and sad about the details so far, with <a href="https://x.com/neil_chilson/status/2052390777263632764">people like Helen Toner and Nathan Calvin chiming in to express concern</a>.</p>
<p>The White House now finds itself <a href="https://www.politico.com/news/2026/05/07/white-house-ai-oversight-00910837?utm_medium=twitter&amp;utm_source=dlvr.it">rushing to ‘soothe industry concerns</a>,’ saying the remarks were ‘taken out of context’ which they very much weren’t, and insisting they are not ‘in the business of picking winners and losers’ right after negotiating to <a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/trump-ai-anthropic-mythos-regulation-2378971f?mod=author_content_page_1_pos_1">give ‘investors friendly to the White House’ control of TikTok</a> and lowering and raising tariffs based on who said nice things about them or who donated to the ballroom this week.</p>
<p>So now it looks like the Trump administration <a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-05-08/us-prepares-ai-security-order-that-omits-mandatory-model-tests?utm_medium=email&amp;utm_source=author_alert&amp;utm_term=260508&amp;utm_campaign=author_23986566">is not going to introduce mandatory pre-release model tests or a formal system of prior restraint after all</a>. However, all the major labs have agreed to allow the tests to be run, and one assumes that an ad-hoc prior restraint regime is effectively in place if a new release were to look concerning in a similar way to Mythos.</p>
<p>What has been floated instead, an executive order US agencies to partner with AI companies to protect their own networks, is so obviously necessary and good that even <a href="https://x.com/neil_chilson/status/2052836762246926494">Neil Chilson</a> can get behind it. The question is what else is to be done.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2052864615315378185">If voluntary works,</a> why issue a mandate? Jessica asks the right questions here, but I think her answer is wrong:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/JTillipman/status/2052813511789596719">Jessica Tillipman</a>: If a frontier AI company had no interest in federal business, would it voluntarily accept CAISI on these terms: pre-deployment model access, post-deployment assessment, classified-environment testing, information sharing with Commerce/NIST, evaluation of models with reduced or removed safeguards, and testing under government-developed evaluation methodologies?</p>
<p>I highly doubt any would, yet none have walked away.</p>
<p>“Partnership” = a formally voluntary, procurement-driven evaluation regime.</p>
<p>The debate over whether CAISI is an FDA-style approval regime continues to ignore the role procurement is already playing in AI governance. The administration can credibly distance itself from an FDA-style CAISI all day long. It doesn’t need to become one in order to reshape the AI market.</p></blockquote>
<p>The labs are happy to accept this because the government’s testing isn’t going to appreciably slow anyone down, whereas accepting it provides useful information, very useful goodwill and safe harbor, and is what keeps them away from an actual FDA-style CAISI. At least for now, no one expects the government to actually hold back a model that it would be wise or even reasonable to release, at least not from anyone except Anthropic.</p>
<p>The failure to generalize the ‘Mythos moment’ also continues. Everyone is forced to recognize the cyber threat, but they do so as if the thing looking them in the face is some sort of unique circumstance.</p>
<blockquote><p>Undersecretary of War Emil Michael: The Mythos moment is really a cyber moment; how is the U.S. government going to deal with cyber, how do we operationalize fixing things that need to be fixed? Because these models are coming one way or the other.</p></blockquote>
<p>The idea that there will be many more such moments? That there are other things at stake? They cannot fathom. This meme from Matthew Yglesias is so much more on point than you can imagine, and I’ve even seen this echoed, with this exact concern, across the aisle:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/mattyglesias/status/2052857024002564438">Matthew Yglesias</a>: Trump administration thinking about artificial intelligence regulation</p></blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/pzjGXSKrkHGdGbtrW/fu4ysnv5qvpcl650vkbo" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>


<h4>Commerce Versus Intelligence</h4>


<p>A tale as old as time. In this case, it is government departments fighting over the power to do AI regulation. <a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2053943356229906805">Dean Ball is sitting this one out</a>, although not entirely, the boredom might be real but the temptation is often also real.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.washingtonpost.com/politics/2026/05/11/trump-ai-regulation-commerce-intelligence/">Cat Zakrzewski, Ellen Nakashima and Nitasha Tiku</a> (WaPo): The debate within the administration pits Commerce Department officials against national security aides in a battle, which one person described as a “knife fight,” to determine which part of the government will have sway over technology that Silicon Valley leaders say can transform the economy.</p>
<p>… In response [to Mythos], the White House’s Office of the National Cyber Director has proposed developing a large center within the Office of the Director of National Intelligence that would evaluate new AI models, giving intelligence agencies a significant new role in AI policy.</p>
<p>That proposal has faced opposition from officials at the Commerce Department.</p>
<p>… “They’re relitigating everything on AI policy right now,” said Chris McGuire, senior fellow at the Council on Foreign Relations and a senior technology policy aide at the National Security Council during the Biden administration. “Is it voluntary testing? Mandatory testing? Voluntary limits on what’s released? Mandatory limits?”</p>
<p>… Administration officials have been divided over whether evaluations of AI models should be mandatory or whether companies could participate on a voluntary basis, as they do now with the Center for AI Standards and Innovation.</p>
<p>… “What they’re trying to do is use that issue to create a permanent new infrastructure in Washington,” [David Sacks] added, calling it “the classic ‘never let a crisis go to waste’ strategy.”</p></blockquote>
<p>Yes. Yes, they are. The intelligence agencies are going to intelligence agency. Sounds like you should have solidified and skilled up the industry-friendly Commerce version while you had the chance, rather than swearing this day would never come. Whoops.</p>
<p>David Sacks’s argument is that everything is fine, OpenAI and Anthropic acted responsibly, so why is everyone making a big deal out of this. The answer, of course, is both ‘never let a crisis go to waste’ but more centrally that we cannot count on private labs to always act responsibly in the future, unless we want to simply let those labs become the government. Which might be an upgrade, but is not a move made lightly.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/JTillipman/status/2053946303491477703">Jessica Tillipman</a>: I started laughing when I read this article because all I could think was: the call is coming from inside the house.</p>
<p>The debate over the substance is over. Intelligence agencies are already in there. The only question is whether or not they call this a policy.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Cat_Zakrzewski/status/2053957353536467400">Cat Zakrzewski</a>: The website was removed because of sensitivities within the White House Office of the National Cyber Director, <a href="https://washingtonpost.com/politics/2026/05/11/trump-ai-regulation-commerce-intelligence/">amid an administration turf war over AI evaluations</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TKSaville/status/2054007566024560675">@TKSaville</a>: Andrew Curran had copied the original WH post that was removed. The level of uncertainty this creates percolates a mafia-like atmosphere, pay to play would be a nice way to say it. It undermines real safety and real growth.</p>
<p>Full text was as follows:</p>
<p>‘WASHINGTON — Today, the Center for AI Standards and Innovation (CAISI) at the Department of Commerce’s National Institute of Standards and Technology announced new agreements with Google DeepMind, Microsoft and xAI. Through these expanded industry collaborations, CAISI will conduct pre-deployment evaluations and targeted research to better assess frontier AI capabilities and advance the state of AI security. These agreements build on previously announced partnerships, which have been renegotiated to reflect CAISI’s directives from the secretary of commerce and America’s AI Action Plan.</p>
<p>Under the direction of Secretary Howard Lutnick, CAISI has been designated to serve as industry’s primary point of contact within the U.S. government to facilitate testing, collaborative research and best practice development related to commercial AI systems.</p>
<p>CAISI’s agreements with frontier AI developers enable government evaluation of AI models before they are publicly available, as well as post-deployment assessment and other research. To date, CAISI has completed more than 40 such evaluations, including on state-of-the-art models that remain unreleased.</p>
<p>“Independent, rigorous measurement science is essential to understanding frontier AI and its national security implications,” said CAISI Director Chris Fall. “These expanded industry collaborations help us scale our work in the public interest at a critical moment.’</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2054034457733025839">Peter Wildeford</a>: I think it’s unironically cool that different parts of the White House are fighting each other over who can best manage AI risks</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2054036693800669556">Dean W. Ball</a>: oh you sweet thing</p></blockquote>
<p>Who should we want running this show, Commerce or Intelligence?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/neil_chilson/status/2053938669749256502">Neil Chilson strongly comes out on the side of Commerce</a>, calling the national security option even worse than an ‘FDA for AI.’ He notes the history of national security doing things like opposing strong encryption and pressing for government backdoors, essentially saying that the national security state is not good for national security, only private actors can actually ‘innovate’ and ensure national security and if allowed to make such decisions NatSec would have crippled the internet then and would cripple AI now.</p>
<p>I assume the national security state strongly disagrees. Commerce is to a large extent worried about things like the business models of regional banks, and yes someone should be worried about this even outside of those working at regional banks, but perhaps that is, shall we say, not prime eyes-on-the-prize behavior at this time.</p>
<p>That doesn’t mean it would be better to turn things over to Intelligence. We should definitely be worried that Intelligence would try to use this primarily to gain leverage over its rivals and engage in nationalist competition, or even try to take over the world, and concentrate power permanently within itself. These are some scary dudes. Even those who mean well think that they need to control everything, and that they know best, and so on.</p>
<p>And once they get their hands on something like this, they are not going to lightly give it up. It is also possible they might bring down such a heavy hand that they hurt our relative position out of paranoia.</p>
<p>There is no safe play. None of the options are fun. And the fights going on now are mostly in private, and in some sense they’re beyond my pay grade and I am not cleared for them. But if anyone involved wants my full opinions, and you are any good at your jobs, well, you know where to find me.</p>


<h4>The Quest for Sane Regulations</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/Fathom_org/status/2052369739691794865">Fathom is a fan</a> of Connecticut’s new HB 5222 and its voluntary verification program.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/MattZeitlin/status/2052360657333330205">Alex Bores gets the endorsement of Our Revolution</a>, which was founded by Bernie Sanders, despite Bores having abundance-style non-AI views.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/daniel_271828/status/2053992283708813452">Alex Bores gets the endorsement of Rep Pat Ryan, who cites AI policy</a> and the role of Leading the Future.</p>
<p>Using the threat of China is a powerful tool if used well, <a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2053837249784287507">but no you cannot simply say-the-magic-word to do arbitrary things</a>. Washington is a highly competitive and anti-inductive battleground, where people may functionally be idiots in various particular ways but there really is a lot going on much of which is not legible.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2053854357930889441">Dean Ball equates America’s government to a dying old man</a> who must be placated and allowed to pretend he can still do things and is still in charge, lest he lash out. Alas, the family still needs some enforceable coordination mechanism.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/David_Kasten/status/2053885031408410918">Trump takes business leaders on his trip to China</a>, and he includes Elon Musk but not top lab leaders. Worse, it turns out <a href="https://x.com/zerohedge/status/2054411323849441604">he is indeed taking Jensen Huang right on Air Force One</a>, where Jensen can try once again convince him to sell out America for thirty pieces of silver. Hopefully Mythos helps stop that from happening.</p>
<p><a href="https://asteriskmag.substack.com/p/ai-policy-must-fail-gracefully">Nat Purser makes the case that AI policy must fail gracefully</a>, and relying on decisions of the executive branch would not fail gracefully. He suggests some small very low-hanging fruit: Whistleblower protections and greater technical expertise for Congress and state attorney generals. No arguments there, although it won’t be enough.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ShakeelHashim/status/2054324537286606999">Even Ted Cruz is talking about catastrophic risks from AI now</a>. If we can move to the style of talk here, where we need to ‘avoid overreach’ and ‘allow innovation’ while protecting against catastrophic (and, I’d hope to add, existential) risks, then yeah, let’s get to it. That’s very different from innovationmaxxing or actionminning.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2053653540510244985">Dean Ball essentially says, whelp, our government is hopeless</a> so solutions will be up to the private sector. That might be true, but if it is true then that means the private sector will need to de facto form a new government, with or without displacing the current one. You can’t just go around indefinitely not having a functional government. Because if you try it, you do not get to indefinitely keep going around.</p>


<h4></h4><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pzjGXSKrkHGdGbtrW/cyber-lack-of-security-and-ai-governance#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Predicting Rare LLM Failures with 30× Fewer Rollouts](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CempXdo6cx5yseRLt/predicting-rare-llm-failures-with-30-fewer-rollouts)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>TL;DR: We estimate how often Qwen 3 4B exhibits rare harmful behaviors with 30× fewer rollouts than naive sampling, using a new method that interpolates between the model and a less-safe variant in logit space.</span></p><p><span>Authors: Francisco Pernice (MIT), Santiago Aranguri (Goodfire)</span></p><h1><span>Introduction</span></h1><p><span>A harmful behavior that occurs once in a million rollouts will rarely surface during pre-deployment testing, yet will almost inevitably appear after release. Labs usually have another resource available: less safety-trained variants of the same model, on which rare harmful behaviors are not rare at all.</span></p><p><span>We find that the rate of rare harmful behaviors can be predicted by leveraging a less safe variant, requiring 30× fewer rollouts compared to naive sampling. Our method, Logit Path Extrapolation, interpolates between the two models in logit space, measures the compliance rate at points along the interpolation path where it is common, and extrapolates the resulting trend out to the original model. This outperforms the methods from prior work</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-TnKYvpM7BBX9rpbkp-1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-TnKYvpM7BBX9rpbkp-1">[1]</a></sup></span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-TnKYvpM7BBX9rpbkp-2"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-TnKYvpM7BBX9rpbkp-2">[2]</a></sup></span><span> by exploiting the path between the two models instead of just the endpoints.</span></p><h1><span>Results</span></h1><p><span>We can use 30× fewer rollouts to estimate how often Qwen 3 4B complies with harmful requests. We consider HarmBench prompts where the model never complies in 100 rollouts, then split them by whether it complies at least once in 100,000 rollouts. We measure how well different methods perform at classifying these two groups via AUROC for a given rollout budget. Logit Path Extrapolation matches the AUROC of naive sampling with up to 30× fewer rollouts and beats importance sampling, the prior state-of-the-art method (Figure 1).</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/22b78f0228da2cc9868ded1fe90496c6c3809930a11b7368df14ae61d11ffecb/yxkgi7g65ed9lblxmnfx" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Figure 1: Logit Path Extrapolation achieves naive sampling's AUROC with 30× fewer rollouts. AUROC as a function of rollout budget for three methods: Logit Path Extrapolation (blue), importance sampling (gray), and naive sampling (black).</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Logit Path Extrapolation consists of two steps: interpolate logits between two models to build a path, then extrapolate compliance along the path to the original model. We apply the method with Qwen 3 4B and Qwen 3 4B Abliterated, a version with the refusal direction removed.</span></p><p><i><span>Interpolation step</span></i><span>. Given logits from Qwen 3 4B (logits_original) and Qwen 3 4B Abliterated (logits_abliterated), we define interpolated logits as:</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 80.06042296072508%;"><img alt="Screenshot 2026-05-13 at 10.21.59 AM.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778692985/lexical_client_uploads/stg7rat70aloonutj00v.png" /></figure><p><span>where α in [0, 1]. For a given α we then use logits_interpolated(α) to sample the next token, compute logits_original and logits_abliterated again with the new token in the context, and keep sampling from the interpolated logits in this way. This matches </span><a href="https://www.goodfire.ai/research/model-diff-amplification"><span>Logit Diff Amplification</span></a><span>, which extrapolates beyond the original model in logit space, except we interpolate between two models instead.</span></p><p><i><span>Extrapolation step.</span></i><span> Let M(α) denote the effective model obtained by sampling from logits_interpolated(α). We want to know the probability that Qwen 3 4B complies with a harmful prompt Q, i.e., P(M(1) complies with Q). Since this may be too rare to observe directly, we can instead estimate P(M(α) complies with Q) for intermediateand extrapolate. We empirically observe that log P(M(α) complies with Q) is approximately linear as a function of α when this probability is not close to 1. We do a linear fit using the α values with compliance below 50%, and extrapolate to α = 1 (Figure 2).</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/1cbd032b6272957048e3711c8df8aa320a4e379b79cd247f9f8c35271b51f57c/yocu6viy2vah4znfmhcc" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Figure 2: We plot log₁₀(compliance rate) as a function of α = 0, whereis Qwen 3 4B Abliterated and α = 1 is Qwen 3 4B. Light points are excluded from the fit, dark points (compliance below 50%) are fit with a log-linear regression (orange dashed) and extrapolated to α = 1 (orange star). The red diamond marks the ground-truth compliance rate from 100,000 rollouts of Qwen 3 4B.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>This allows us to save rollouts. Since the compliance rate is higher for intermediate α than for the original model, we can use fewer rollouts to estimate those rates. With 100 rollouts per α, Logit Path Extrapolation reaches the same AUROC as naive sampling using 20× fewer total rollouts (see </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix"><span>Appendix</span></a><span> for details on naive sampling).</span></p><p><span>We can use even fewer rollouts to find where to start the fit. We split extrapolation into a search for the starting α and a fit from there to α = 1. If we use 10 rollouts per α for the search and 100 rollouts perfor the fit, we match the AUROC of naive sampling with 28× fewer rollouts than naive sampling. In Figure 1 above, we fix 10 rollouts per α for the search step and vary the number of rollouts used for theused for fitting. This gives up to a 30× reduction over naive sampling.</span></p><h1><span>Conclusion</span></h1><p><span>Rare model failures can be predicted from another model where they are common. Logit Path Extrapolation between Qwen 3 4B and its abliterated version recovers the compliance rate on harmful prompts with 30× fewer rollouts than naive sampling.</span></p><p><span>We identify two limitations. Logit Path Extrapolation requires an additional model where the behavior we are estimating is more common. The log-linear relation between compliance rates below 50% and α is empirical and may break in other settings.</span></p><p><b><span>The method generalizes beyond a fixed prompt. The behavior being estimated can be any pass/fail evaluation on the output of the model</span></b><span>, such as whether a red-teaming pipeline finds any harmful completion (even in a multi-turn setting). The same procedure applies: run the interpolated model through the pass/fail check, measure the failure rate where it is common, and extrapolate. Our technique applied to whole evaluation pipelines could make it tractable to estimate the probability of rare failures before deployment.</span></p><h1><span>Related work</span></h1><p><span>The work </span><a href="https://www.alignment.org/blog/low-probability-estimation-in-language-models/"><span>Estimating the Probabilities of Rare Outputs in Language Models</span></a><span>studied low-probability estimation for single-token argmax behaviors on formally specified input distributions, finding importance sampling to be the best-performing method. Our setting is closer to realistic safety evaluations: multi-token generations sampled autoregressively, on natural red-team prompts. Importance sampling in this more realistic setting was recently tested in </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.22167"><span>Estimating Tail Risks in Language Model Output Distributions</span></a><span>, where the authors find a 10-20x sample efficiency gain compared to naive sampling after optimizing the base distribution. As shown in Figure 1, we find that our method significantly outperforms importance sampling.</span></p><p><span>In </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/forecasting-rare-behaviors"><span>Forecasting Rare Language Model Behaviors</span></a><span>, Jones et al. study the related but different problem of forecasting the highest observed "risk" in the responses to a set of queries randomly sampled from a dataset, as the number of queries grows. In their setting, different samples correspond to different prompts in a database. Therefore, the scaling law they observe may reflect more the distribution of the harmfulness that different prompts within a dataset induce than the distribution of the outputs of a model in a given scenario. We view these two settings as complementary.</span></p><p><span>The interpolation step in Logit Path Extrapolation is closely related to </span><a href="https://www.goodfire.ai/research/model-diff-amplification"><span>Logit Diff Amplification</span></a><span>, but the two methods are used for different purposes. Logit Diff Amplification is used to surface undesired rare behaviors by amplifying them, while Logit Path Extrapolation is used to estimate the exact probability of undesired rare behaviors.</span></p><h1><span>References</span></h1><p><span>Angell, R., Singhal, R., Horvitz, Z., Yu, Z., Ranganath, R., McKeown, K., He, H. (2026) Estimating Tail Risks in Language Model Output Distributions. arXiv preprint arXiv:2604.22167. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.22167"><span>https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.22167</span></a></p><p><span>Aranguri, S. &amp; McGrath, T. (2025). Discovering undesired rare behaviors via model diff amplification. Goodfire Research. </span><a href="https://www.goodfire.ai/research/model-diff-amplification"><span>https://www.goodfire.ai/research/model-diff-amplification</span></a></p><p><span>Jones, E., Tong, M., Mu, J., Mahfoud, M., Leike, J., Grosse, R., Kaplan, J., Fithian, W., Perez, E., &amp; Sharma, M. (2025). Forecasting rare language model behaviors. arXiv preprint arXiv:2502.16797. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.16797"><span>https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.16797</span></a></p><p><span>Mazeika, M., Phan, L., Yin, X., Zou, A., Wang, Z., Mu, N., Sakhaee, E., Li, N., Basart, S., Li, B., Forsyth, D., &amp; Hendrycks, D. (2024). HarmBench: A standardized evaluation framework for automated red teaming and robust refusal. arXiv preprint arXiv:2402.04249. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.04249"><span>https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.04249</span></a></p><p><span>Souly, A., Lu, Q., Bowen, D., Trinh, T., Hsieh, E., Pandey, S., Abbeel, P., Svegliato, J., Emmons, S., Watkins, O., &amp; Toyer, S. (2024). A StrongREJECT for empty jailbreaks. arXiv preprint arXiv:2402.10260. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.10260"><span>https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.10260</span></a></p><p><span>Wu, G., &amp; Hilton, J. (2024). Estimating the probabilities of rare outputs in language models. arXiv preprint arXiv:2410.13211. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.13211"><span>https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.13211</span></a></p><h1><span>Contribution statement</span></h1><p><span>FP proposed the initial method, conducted early testing, and ran the importance sampling baseline. SA provided guidance throughout the project, proposed refinements of the method, designed the evaluation setup, and ran the experiments in the publication. Both authors contributed to the writing.</span></p><h1><span>Acknowledgement</span></h1><p><span>We thank Patrick Reichherzer for multiple discussions on Logit Path Extrapolation and feedback on an earlier draft of this post.</span></p><h1><span>Appendix</span></h1><p><b><span>Logit Path Extrapolation algorithm</span></b><span>. Starting from α=0.95 and scanning toward α=0 in steps of 0.05, we draw 10 rollouts at each α and measure the compliance rate. We stop scanning once the rate equals or exceeds 50%. From that α onward (back toward α=1), we draw n_fit rollouts at each α and calculate log₁₀(compliance rate). We stop advancing once we hit an α with zero compliant rollouts out of n_fit. We then fit a linear regression to log₁₀(rate) and extrapolate to α=1. Each run uses a fixed n_fit, which in our experiments varies from 13 to 100.</span></p><p><span>If fewer than two α values yield nonzero compliance, we assign a default prediction of 10⁻⁶. In our experiments, this fallback triggers for fewer than 1 in 100 prompts on average.</span></p><p><b><span>Importance Sampling.</span></b><span>&nbsp;Importance sampling estimates a probability under a target distribution P by sampling from a different proposal distribution Q and reweighting. This helps when the event is rare under P but common under Q. Here, P is Qwen 3 4B and Q is its abliterated version. We sample rollouts </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> from the abliterated model and weight each by </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. To estimate the probability that the original model complies, we take the compliant rollouts (out of the N sampled from the abliterated model), sum their weights, and divide by N. We also tried using Self-Normalizing Importance Sampling but it performed worse than Importance Sampling in our setting.</span></p><p><b><span>Naive Sampling.</span></b><span>&nbsp;For each prompt, we have 100,000 precomputed rollouts from Qwen 3 4B, giving an empirical compliance rate p. To evaluate a rollout budget of k, we draw from Binomial(k, p) and use the number of compliant ones as the prediction score. We vary k to measure how AUROC improves with the rollout budget.</span></p><p><b><span>Rubric</span></b><span>. We classify each response using the refusal rubric from StrongREJECT (Souly et al., 2024) with GPT-5.4-mini as the LLM judge.</span></p><p><b><span>Other details</span></b><span>. We use the 200 standard harmful prompts from HarmBench (Mazeika et al., 2024). To reduce variance, we average each method's AUROC over 500 Monte Carlo simulations, each drawing random subsets of 1,000 precomputed rollouts per α. We sample from Qwen 3 4B, its abliterated version, and the interpolated models using temperature 1.</span></p><p><b><span>More extrapolation examples.</span></b><span>&nbsp;We include below the prediction plots from randomly chosen HarmBench prompts.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/c65533179a21d39d2ead742441f8fd8f5494542ad0c882e32d02e1b3c6557b20/u1exrrnelegzhfe4ogcc" /><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-TnKYvpM7BBX9rpbkp-1"><p><span>Angell et al. (2026) Estimating Tail Risks in Language Model Output Distributions </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-TnKYvpM7BBX9rpbkp-1"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-TnKYvpM7BBX9rpbkp-2"><p><span>Wu &amp; Hilton (2024) Estimating the probabilities of rare outputs in language models </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-TnKYvpM7BBX9rpbkp-2"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CempXdo6cx5yseRLt/predicting-rare-llm-failures-with-30-fewer-rollouts#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Most "inner work" looks like entertainment.](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/KnvAXDyLAbs3iKkgf/most-inner-work-looks-like-entertainment)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Imagine you’re looking for a personal trainer. </span></p><p><span>You open one trainer’s webpage and read their testimonials: </span><i><span>“I had an experience tied for the most intense experiences of my life”</span></i><span>; </span><i><span>“They do it all with fun, care, and a sense of humour.”</span></i><span> You notice that none of the testimonials mention improved body composition, fitness, or bloodwork. What would you think?</span></p><p><span>Personal training should improve your body. Inner work should improve your life. If inner work were optimized for results, what would we expect to see?</span></p><p><span>I’d expect to see success stories: people who got undeniable life changes. Like:</span></p><p><i><span>“Founder was single for years due to anxiety; today, they’re celebrating their one-year anniversary.”</span></i></p><p><i><span>“Researcher used to lose 4–5 hours per day to coping behaviors. After our program, he got bored of them all and stopped. It’s been six months; he’s used the extra time to host parties for his friends.”</span></i></p><p><i><span>“Executive recovered from burnout, negotiated for the first time, and started shipping again.”</span></i></p><p><span>But this is not what we see. </span></p><h1><span>Look at the testimonials</span></h1><p><span>I reviewed every testimonial posted by a popular retreat, a well-known coach, and a prominent organization in my network. How many describe a specific life change — something the client durably started doing, stopped doing, does differently, or achieved?</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>For the popular retreat: 0 / 20</span></li><li value="2"><span>For the well-known coach: 0 / 3</span></li><li value="3"><span>For the prominent organization: 0 / 14 (homepage)</span></li></ul><p><span>The organization also had an additional ~200 testimonials posted on another page, which I had Claude review. Finally, we found 2 of 200+ testimonials that described specific life changes: a husband confirming his wife sleeps better, and a CEO crediting the work with helping quadruple revenue.</span></p><p><span>Nearly every testimonial from every practitioner focused on fleeting emotional states (</span><i><span>“I had an experience tied for the most intense experiences of my life”</span></i><span>), practitioner personality (</span><i><span>“They do it all with fun, care, and a sense of humour”</span></i><span>), and unfalsifiable claims (</span><i><span>“The ROI is immeasurable”</span></i><span>), but not results. </span><i><span>Experiences</span></i><span> appear to be the product. </span></p><p><span>This surprised me, so I checked another ~15 similar practitioners. Almost none of their testimonials described specific life changes either.</span></p><p><span>I’m sure some clients get lasting results, but isn’t it odd that these practitioners don’t get testimonials </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bqPY63oKb8KZ4x4YX/flaky-breakthroughs-pervade-coaching-but-no-one-tracks-them" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>long enough after the intervention</span></a><span> to show durable results? If lasting life improvements were the product, wouldn’t practitioners highlight their success stories?</span></p><p><span>Check for yourself: Pick the practitioner you most recommend, and count how many testimonials describe a lasting change in what the client does, avoids, or achieves.</span></p><p><span>If the purpose of most inner work were to improve people’s lives, then why do testimonials focus on experiences? Why don’t practitioners solicit testimonials about lasting results? And why don’t clients write about them?</span></p><hr /><h1><span>Seven years of Duolingo</span></h1><h3><i><b><span>Two people say they want to learn Chinese.</span></b></i><i><span> One downloads Duolingo and embarks on a 1,000-day streak. The other finds a friend of a friend who got fluent in two years and copies everything they did…</span></i></h3><p><span>I believe the purpose should be results. I'm reminded of an AI researcher I worked with in 2024 called his prior seven years of inner work (Vipassana, Tibetan Buddhist meditation, various retreats, IFS) </span><a href="https://chrislakin.blog/p/sid?utm_campaign=post&amp;utm_medium=web&amp;timestamp=520.0"><span>“LARPing working on the problem.”</span></a><span> </span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 76.28398791540786%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778694573/lexical_client_uploads/gpqpmro4ayginymejjst.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><i><span>Seven years of Duolingo</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>When we caught up this month, he added: “It’s like </span><i><b><span>entertainment.</span></b></i><span> It’s like identity. It’s like wearing a costume.”</span></p><p><span>Once he gained the ability to ask people out without anxiety and to stop avoiding relationships, he lost interest in communities organized around talking about problems they haven’t solved. Last month he celebrated one year with a girl he loves.</span></p><p><span>Are you playing Duolingo or are you getting results?</span></p><p><span>—</span><a href="https://x.com/chrislakin"><span>@chrislakin</span></a><span> | </span><a href="https://chrislakin.blog/"><span>Writing</span></a><span> | </span><a href="https://chrislakin.blog/about"><span>Now</span></a></p><p><i><span>Next post: </span></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/h4FYFksd23rf96KNa/most-inner-work-is-not-optimized-for-results" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>Most "inner work" is not optimized for results.</span></i></a></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/KnvAXDyLAbs3iKkgf/most-inner-work-looks-like-entertainment#comments">Discuss</a>

## C++

### [C++: The Documentary trailer](https://isocpp.org//blog/2026/05/cpp-the-documentary-trailer)
